## AI Research

### [A Chinese Perspective on “On Social Reality in China”](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/m4fWCPqWkpb39SKi7/a-chinese-perspective-on-on-social-reality-in-china-2)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/b5cSYh4emQb2qrGmK/on-social-reality-in-china" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“On Social Reality in China”</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> makes many accurate observations, but I find the comparison fundamentally uneven. There are already many interesting comments there, but they’re too scattered, so I’m starting a separate post to respond in a more systematic and comprehensive way.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The most critical blind spot is this: the author draws on his familiarity with Western liberal middle-class intellectual circles, yet primarily understands China through pop culture and the experiences of the previous generation of immigrants. This is hardly fair. China has always had many small, highly self-selected communities of intellectuals, much like LessWrong itself. Since we’re looking for rationalists—regardless of which country they come from—why judge them by their country’s pop culture?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’ll respond to the author’s four axes one by one, then discuss why rationalist and EA communities are harder to build in China. Briefly, I believe that censorship mechanisms and speech controls affect independent organizations far more than China’s broader cultural context does. Under these circumstances, even when people with similar interests and beliefs come together, it’s difficult for them to build anything—I’ve seen far too many examples.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">About me: born in China in the mid-1990s, grew up in a middle-class family in one of the major cities, and might be on the autism spectrum. I moved to Western Europe before the pandemic and now live in France. My perspective is limited too, but I know many Chinese intellectual communities that care about knowledge, truth, social justice, and technology ethics.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Contents</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1. Chinese social media is like social media everywhere.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2. “The primacy of social reality” is not relevant here.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3. “The Dark World frame” is not a fixed cultural phenomenon, but a product of its time, and it’s changing.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">4. “Chinese nationalism as collective insecurity” is the section I agree with the most—let’s take a deeper look.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">5. In China, people with backgrounds in the humanities and social sciences are more enthusiastic about engaging in public discourse than those with backgrounds in STEM. Continue to produce high-quality translations—even if they don’t help with policymaking, they allow people to see one another.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Chinese Social Media Is Like Social Media Everywhere</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The author looks at mainstream Chinese social media through its lowest-quality content, while his view of the West comes from a very different intellectual environment. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Beauty filters, anxiety about body image, a flood of low-quality books and films, and internet users’ pursuit of exposure and attention—aren’t these things also obviously present on YouTube, Instagram, Reddit, and TikTok? These issues have long been serious topics of research in the US and Europe, and the same is true in China.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jonathan Haidt discussed many of these problems in his </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Atlantic</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> article </span><a href="https://www.theatlantic.com/magazine/archive/2022/05/social-media-democracy-trust-babel/629369/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Why the Past 10 Years of American Life Have Been Uniquely Stupid.”</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> He describes how likes, shares, and recommendation algorithms reward emotional content and encourage people to perform for audiences. Chinese media have been criticizing these problems too. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sanlian Life Weekly</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> has published in-depth reports on the logic of capital behind the mini-drama industry and the phenomena it has created: </span><a href="https://www.lifeweek.com.cn/article/217248"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“How Do Viral Mini-Dramas Spark a Traffic Frenzy?”</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and the podcast episode </span><a href="https://podcasts.apple.com/hk/podcast/ep123-%E7%9F%AD%E5%89%A7%E6%B5%81%E6%B0%B4%E7%BA%BF%E4%B8%8A-%E4%B8%8D%E5%8F%AA%E6%98%AF%E7%94%9C%E5%AE%A0-%E8%B5%98%E5%A9%BF-%E9%80%86%E8%A2%AD%E9%87%8D%E7%94%9F/id1605725063?i=1000641516458"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“The Mini-Drama Assembly Line.”</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> In China’s intellectual circles, such reflections are by no means uncommon.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Critiquing low-quality popular entertainment is a timeless topic among intellectuals worldwide. In today’s world, if you can use high-quality content to deconstruct such extremely widespread and absurd material, you can also attract a large audience and profit from it. China has no shortage of such content creators and influencers.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There is no evidence to suggest that the widespread problems caused by social media are more severe in China than elsewhere. But more importantly, what do any of these observations tell us about the people rationalist communities hope to reach? LessWrong didn't turn itself into Twitter to attract more readers, did it?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I suspect the author would never actively seek out the most superficial content on Western social media, just as I would never seek out the Chinese content he describes. Chinese is my first language, so I know how to find media, podcasts, and intellectual communities that align with my interests, and such resources are not scarce. However, seeking out, discerning, and consistently following these sources of information requires a considerable level of language proficiency and cultural familiarity. For overseas Chinese who primarily engage with China through family experiences and pop culture, this is neither easy nor, it seems, necessary.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Of course, Chinese and Western social media are not identical, especially given China’s strict censorship. But I would never go into a Chinese intellectual community and claim that Fox News and Instagram influencers represent American society. I'll spare you the screenshots.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Primacy of Social Reality: How Is This Relevant?</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Many of the things the author describes are real, but I don’t see how they are particularly relevant to finding rationalists in China. Still, I’d like to correct a few of his interpretations. I’ll leave some detailed points in the comments, since they would take us too far from the main theme.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I haven’t read </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The 48 Laws of Power</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, so I won’t comment on the book itself. But in fact, there has never been a shortage of reflection on the ideas this book seeks to convey. For centuries, literature from around the world—in English, French, Chinese, and other languages—has never lacked critical works addressing social structures, power dynamics, and manipulation. Think of Jane Austen, Edith Wharton, Guy de Maupassant, or Lu Xun. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I do think that in East Asian societies such as China, Japan, and South Korea, the pressure stemming from “social norms” is stronger than in Western societies. However, there are significant differences across regions, just as there are within the West—for example, Britain is also known for its own strong traditions of social restraint. Still, none of this is relevant here.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don’t know whether China’s current political system is historically inevitable (who knows?), but we cannot simply confuse “people’s circumstances” with “people’s beliefs.” Understanding these rules, using them (whether actively or passively), believing in them, and having the ability to change them—these are entirely different matters. If my mom said “Americans just love Trump,” I would definitely correct her.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The same goes for unspoken rules. Drinking culture, workplace hierarchies, bribery, and nepotism are all common in China. But when I watched </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">House of Cards</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Succession</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, those power games felt all too familiar. Still, I don’t see why any of this should matter when looking for rationalists. When I talk to people in rationalist communities, I’m interested in their ideas, not their position in some social hierarchy. I would never try to understand American rationalists by studying the social hierarchy of college fraternities or Wall Street.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In this regard, the differences between various industries and organizations are far more significant than geographical cultural differences. For example, drinking culture and relationship-based business practices can hardly be seen in Shenzhen’s tech industry, often called China’s Silicon Valley. I doubt most people in rationalist communities work in traditional industries, so why use traditional workplace culture to understand potential rationalists in China?</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Dark World Frame: A Product of Its Time</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I do agree that the Dark World Frame the author describes is quite common in China, probably more so than in many Western countries. But I don’t think it’s a fixed feature of Chinese culture. This worldview has been strongly shaped by particular historical and economic conditions, and it is changing now.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I agree with w_jin’s comment about how economic reforms and mass migration disrupted traditional networks of trust in China. See Fei Xiaotong’s </span><a href="https://www.ucpress.edu/books/from-the-soil/paper" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From the Soil From the Soil: The Foundations of Chinese Society</span></i></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which describes how social relationships and trust worked in traditional rural China. His </span><a href="https://press.uchicago.edu/ucp/books/book/chicago/C/bo3621113.html" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">China’s Gentry: Essays on Rural-Urban Relations</span></i></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is also worth reading for its analysis of how China’s traditional social structures were changing long before these reforms:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Unfortunately China is still in the status of a semicolony. Large-scale industrial development is not feasible. The essence of Western civilization, industrialism, has not been really introduced as yet. What has been brought in is the superstructure of the more superficial elements of this civilization, including the desire for material comforts and satisfactions. But there has not been constructed as yet the material foundation on the basis of which these things would make sense. This lack of foundation creates a special class of people who live superficially in Western culture but without a traditional base from either the West or the East. These are both the victims and the directors of China’s tragedy.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">— Fei Xiaotong, “Social Erosion in the Rural Communities,” in </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">China’s Gentry: Essays on Rural-Urban Relations</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (Chicago: University of Chicago Press, 1953), pp. 139–140.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That was written between 1947 and 1948. Beyond Fei’s analysis, I’d like to add some more historical context. In 1949, the Communist Party won the civil war and founded the People’s Republic of China—the China we know today. In the following years, the communist ideal provided many people with a framework for understanding fairness, personal sacrifice, and collectivism. Among the intellectuals who came of age in the 1930s and 1940s, many sincerely believed that a better world could be built through this new social system.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At that time, the Soviet Union had not yet collapsed, and resisting capitalism and realizing communism remained important goals for China. As for the phrase “saving lives and helping people”—for which the author’s wife was mocked by their Chinese friend—this spirit of universal love was precisely the kind of value that the government was promoting at the time.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But then came the Great Leap Forward and the Cultural Revolution, which caused enormous human suffering, and many people began to question the relationship between political ideals and reality. They paid a terrible price for different visions of a better world.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Then, in 1978, China began its economic reforms. Over the following decades, people saw themselves or those around them earning much more money, eating food they had never been able to afford, moving into better homes, buying cars, and traveling. My parents lived through all of this, and I grew up surrounded by people whose lives and values were shaped by these changes. I imagine the author's parents may have had similar experiences.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That generation tends to emphasize the role of individual effort in wealth accumulation, yet overlooks the fact that they are beneficiaries of the tide of history. Rapid social mobility created countless exciting opportunities, and the wealth gap widened rapidly. Compared to previous decades, economic success suddenly became the most attractive goal.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There is a form of intergenerational rebellion at play. The generation before them once believed in grand social ideals, only to experience social upheaval and the shattering of those ideals. As a result, the younger generation has reached a completely different conclusion: What good are ideals? Making money is what really matters. Rather than discussing how society should be, it’s better to focus on how to improve one’s own life.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This reminds me of the American Dream. When people believe they have a chance to become winners, they may accept the rules of competition even if those rules produce enormous inequality.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And in China, the cost of staying in that competition is enormous. I’m getting tired of explaining to my European friends that China’s education and work culture have long demanded far more time and effort than they’re used to. Students grow up with long hours of schoolwork, and adults face a widespread culture of working overtime. The right to rest, which European workers fought for over generations, is still poorly protected in China. For generations of Chinese people, this has been a normal part of life.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But this worldview changes as economic conditions change. In recent years, I’ve seen more discussion of workers’ rights, inequality, gender, and mental health in Chinese-language media. These concerns aren’t new; however, as the economy has slowed, such views have become easier to spread. The next round of intergenerational rebellion is just beginning.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Chinese Nationalism as Collective Insecurity: A Deeper Look</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I agree that Chinese nationalism involves a strong sense of collective insecurity and a desire to prove China’s worth to the West. As a side note, I think China and Russia share many similarities in this regard. I’ve previously discussed the historical roots of Russian nationalism in a Chinese-language podcast, based on my research into the evolution of Russian architecture. Why not just discuss China directly? Because it would most likely be blocked by censorship. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So, let me take this opportunity to talk a little about China. China suffered numerous foreign invasions in the 19th century. I don’t think I need to denounce the history of colonialism to a Western liberal intellectual community that values self-reflection so highly, do I?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In middle and high school history classes, we were taught about the immense cultural treasures of China’s millennia-long history, and then about how humiliating it was to be invaded by Western countries. This narrative is, of course, intended to reinforce a sense of national identity, as history education does in many countries. Building on this foundation, political classes emphasized the conflict between foreign capitalism and our socialism.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hence we were constantly reminded of how China had been humiliated by Western powers, and how we needed to become strong so we wouldn’t be bullied again. At the same time, however, Western countries were always viewed as more advanced in terms of civilization and standard of living. Going to the US to study advanced technology, or working for an international company, was considered a remarkable achievement in life. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is, of course, a very paradoxical phenomenon. On the one hand, we emphasize how great we’ve been throughout history; on the other hand, people yearn for a better life abroad.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This situation has changed significantly in recent years. With China’s economic and technological progress, as well as the deterioration of its relationship with the US, more and more people have come to believe that life in China is better than in the West. This phenomenon is certainly linked to government propaganda, but it is also tied to actual improvements in the quality of life.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I sometimes use this slightly silly analogy: a child who was bullied growing up finally becomes strong, yet still holds resentment over what he went through, so he longs to prove his strength to everyone. His mindset has shifted from an inferiority complex to a superiority complex, but deep down, he has never really lost that insecurity. I must emphasize that, although this is a common mindset, there are also lots of Chinese people who do not think this way.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">China’s emphasis on science and technology is also closely tied to this period of history. Back then, China was invaded first by Britain and later by British and French forces. Our significant technological and military backwardness led the Qing government to conclude that we must learn from Western technology to strengthen our own capabilities. From then on, science and technology became closely linked to national dignity. Today’s AI race continues this pattern: it is a contest of national power.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But this is clearly not unique to China; the US also views AI as part of its national power. The competition left over from the Cold War does not seem to have disappeared, and technology companies and research institutions in both countries face not only commercial competition but also the responsibility to contribute to their nations. The issue of AI safety naturally takes a back seat.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now here’s the crucial difference that I think everyone knows: researchers in China and the US have completely different levels of freedom of speech. (We complain about it privately, cry a little, and then get back to working overtime.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Even those Chinese who sincerely fear that AI will destroy humanity may worry: Will people think I'm siding with the US? Think of Oppenheimer during the Cold War, when his objections to hydrogen bomb development became matters of national security. A scientist’s concerns about a technology can become a question of political loyalty. Of course, Americans today seem much less worried about being accused of siding with China. After all, where could possibly be better than America?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Not every Chinese researcher faces the same situation. But if rationalist communities that can speak freely want to connect with AI safety researchers in China, understanding these practical constraints is more important than speculating about the extent of their nationalist sentiments.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Looking for Rationalists in China: Where Should We Start?</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Back to the original question: how do we connect?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’d like to introduce something that may be unfamiliar to Western readers. Before 2014, China’s education system divided academic subjects into two rigid categories. Starting in our second year of high school, students were split into the “science track” and the “humanities track.” All students took Chinese, math, and English; the science track added physics, chemistry, and biology, while the humanities track added politics, history, and geography. This directly determined which majors we could choose in college. For example, if you chose the humanities track, you could not major in computer science.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This division began to be gradually reformed after 2014, but it wasn’t until 2025 that the changes took effect in most provinces. It is still too early to tell what impact this has had, so the phenomenon I’m about to describe may belong to an era that is coming to an end.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The science–humanities binary has had a profound impact on how people think. From the age of sixteen, students on the two tracks studied different subjects and were set on different career paths, and many came to see themselves as two different kinds of people. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Given this divide, I think we shouldn’t limit our focus to China’s tech professionals; we should also consider those in the humanities and social sciences. This is based on my own experience, of course. My background is in architecture, and I follow discussions from both side. I’ve come across plenty of discussions about AI risks in Chinese-language podcasts and in-depth media; they talk about how algorithms affect workers, the power of tech companies, inequality, and the ethics of technology.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I know these aren’t exactly the same concerns as AI alignment and existential risk, which are major topics in rationalist communities right now. Discussions of AI in China’s social science circles have more in common with how </span><a href="https://youtu.be/S18jxWZntUo?si=YJgNwsbfPEaFlV45"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Daily Show</span></i></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> approaches AI. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But what exactly are we looking for? People who already agree with LessWrong’s perspective on AI safety, or people who are willing to seriously discuss knowledge, truth, technological risks, and humanity’s future?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here’s why I have more confidence in those with a broader background in the social sciences: from what I’ve seen, China’s tech industry tends to place more value on technological capabilities and the direct successes these capabilities bring, while inequality and disadvantaged people are easily overlooked. It’s not necessarily hostility: “I don’t look down on you. I just don’t actually see you.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Silicon Valley also has its own version of tech elitism, but, again, China has political restrictions on public discussion. When talking about social issues brings little professional reward and may carry political risks, people tend to focus on topics that are both safe and useful for their careers. That doesn’t mean they don’t care about anything else.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The humanities and social sciences, however, have a different intellectual tradition, where questions of fairness, power dynamics, and social responsibility are central. Despite all the restrictions, people continue to discuss these issues through research, writing, podcasts, and other channels, often with no choice but to keep the discussions private or semi-public.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Allow me to recommend </span><a href="https://theinitium.com/"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Initium Media (</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">端传媒</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">)</span></i></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, a Chinese-language publication that does excellent in-depth reporting on social and political issues. It operates outside mainland China, which gives it more freedom to cover topics that are heavily censored there.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, Chinese liberals don’t always share the same priorities as their Western counterparts, which can sometimes make communication between them difficult.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In China, people who care about social justice are still dealing with many unresolved issues. A widely discussed 2020 investigation by </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Renwu (People)</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> magazine, </span><a href="https://chinadigitaltimes.net/chinese/654944.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Delivery Riders, Trapped in the System,”</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> examined how delivery algorithms put workers under dangerous pressure. There are also issues involving discrimination against women, pressure on students, intergenerational trauma, LGBTQ rights, and so on. Even discussing these issues openly can come at a high cost. And then there’s the most fundamental question: How do we find ways to get our message across despite strict censorship?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My Western friends sometimes say to me, “We’re dealing with these problems too, but climate change and existential AI risks also need urgent attention.” I understand that. But there are still so many basic social issues on which we have made far less progress than Western countries have. Looking back through history, we can see that the issues people worry about and hope to address vary across different stages of social development.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This raises another question: even if more Chinese people become concerned about existential AI risks, how much influence could they have on national AI safety policy?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In Western countries, discussions among the public and professionals have the potential to eventually influence national policy. It doesn’t work like that in China. Environmental policy is a good example: environmental measures are driven primarily by top-down policy goals from the government, not by independent environmental movements or voluntary corporate action.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So if the goal is to influence China’s AI safety policy directly, rationalist communities will need to find ways to reach people involved in policymaking. That said, it is possible that people within the Chinese government are already doing this, as some commenters have suggested. I’m not on the front line, so I don’t know any more than that. </span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Closing Thoughts</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To sum up, I think the biggest barrier to our finding one another is language, not cultural context. Keep translating good work, and people with similar interests and values will eventually connect. I believe projects like </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/GoX3uYQ4QN5HKvL7u/please-give-them-a-chance-on-china-rationalism-and-ai-safety" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Please Give Them a Chance”</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> can help make that happen. I’m skeptical about how much this will influence AI safety in China, but that doesn’t make the effort any less worthwhile. After all, LessWrong didn’t become what it is by trying to fit into mainstream American culture.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Finally, many thanks to </span><a href="https://www.zhihu.com/people/L.M.Sherlock"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thoughts Memo</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/u973rQm56oijS5Adf/launching-babel-translation"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Babel Translation</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and everyone working to make knowledge accessible across languages. I’ve learned so much from translations myself. In China, we sometimes think of these translators as “盗火者”—those who steal fire like Prometheus. May all those who share knowledge find like-minded people.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/m4fWCPqWkpb39SKi7/a-chinese-perspective-on-on-social-reality-in-china-2#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Linkpost: Neural Networks Are Already Clean](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/x4kvabkF2Jk8GLZp8/linkpost-neural-networks-are-already-clean)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>This is an extremely lengthy post by Nick Cammarata.</p>
<p>Author's summary on twitter:</p>
<blockquote>
<p>interpretability might be the most important scientific problem in the world right now, as I don't think we can solve alignment if we don't know what we're building. I think I have good news about it. I co-authored early work in mechanistic interpretability at OpenAI and came away pessimistic, because neural networks appeared to fold into arbitrary tangled knots. years later, I started openai's first large-scale automated interpretability project, finding about 2% of neurons legible with the primitive tools we had</p>
</blockquote>
<blockquote>
<p>with modern agentic tools and a much closer look, the picture looks totally different. the vast majority in models up to trillions of parameters are legible, and their circuits beautiful. many famous examples of tangled neurons turned out to be misunderstood. The roadblocks I expected mostly aren't there. I now think ambitious interpretability will be solved by the time we need it most, and possibly quite soon, as automated scientists keep improving. it may be a bitter lesson for interpretability: the simple thing, at absurd scale, works</p>
</blockquote>
<p>Roon's quote tweet</p>
<blockquote>
<p>when nick told me that he had some interp breakthroughs, he was uh, underselling it. i think this may be remembered as a major moment in scientific history. news so good even eliezer will have to lower his pdoom. we can start making alignment into a 'normal science'</p>
</blockquote>
<blockquote>
<p>I feel like my understanding of what a neural net is has been catapulted into the future by five years or something. it has brought back a lot of the sheer joy I had for studying the mystery of deep learning. neurons are already clean! they form a wonderful alien ontology!</p>
</blockquote>
<p>I'd love to know:</p>
<ol>
<li>How much of a real advance is this, vs things we already knew?</li>
<li>How is this likely to be useful in practice for the purpose of alignment?</li>
</ol>
<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/x4kvabkF2Jk8GLZp8/linkpost-neural-networks-are-already-clean#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Preface to the Computational Agent Theory and ASI Alignment Sequence](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/NQLCdfeNsQHZaJ8iC/preface-to-the-computational-agent-theory-and-asi-alignment)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>The idea of “artificial superintelligence<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-xjTQqf6yndAw2qETs-1">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-xjTQqf6yndAw2qETs-1">[1]</a></sup>
    </span>” (ASI) has recently crossed from the realm of science fiction (in the mainstream perception) to the realm of near-term technological goals openly pursued by most leading commercial organizations in the field of AI. Unfortunately, ASI poses a grave existential risk to the human species. This was argued by Eliezer Yudkowsky since <a href="https://intelligence.org/files/CFAI.pdf">2001</a><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-xjTQqf6yndAw2qETs-2">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-xjTQqf6yndAw2qETs-2">[2]</a></sup>
    </span>, and by many others (see e.g., <a href="https://steveomohundro.com/wp-content/uploads/2009/12/ai_drives_final.pdf">Omohundro 2009</a>, <a href="https://books.google.co.il/books/about/Superintelligence.html?id=7_H8AwAAQBAJ">Bostrom 2014</a>, <a href="https://smarterthan.us/">Armstrong 2014</a>, <a href="https://books.google.co.il/books/about/Human_Compatible.html?id=M1eFDwAAQBAJ">Russell 2019</a>, <a href="https://www.science.org/doi/full/10.1126/science.adn0117">Bengio et al. 2024</a> (25 authors), <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.15657">Bengio et al. 2025</a> (13 authors) and <a href="https://ifanyonebuildsit.com/">Yudkowsky and Soares 2025</a>).</p>
<p>In light of this risk comes the question of how can we make ASI safe<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-xjTQqf6yndAw2qETs-3">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-xjTQqf6yndAw2qETs-3">[3]</a></sup>
    </span>. This question is challenging on many levels, because to solve it we need to complete vast gaps in our understanding both in theoretical computer science and in the areas of philosophy concerned with knowledge and decision-making. Moreover, the cost of failure is so enormous that any sane solution has to meet unprecedented standards of reliability and robustness. Not to mention the sociopolitical problem of balance of power in the post-ASI age, something that is out-of-scope for this sequence.</p>
<p>Personally, I started to be concerned with this problem around 2013. In 2018, I <a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/5bd75cc58225bf0670375575/the-learning-theoretic-ai-alignment-research-agenda">created</a> the learning-theoretic AI alignment research agenda (LTA). The LTA combined ideas from MIRI’s <a href="https://intelligence.org/files/TechnicalAgenda.pdf">agent foundations</a> agenda with the mathematical tools of statistical and computational learning theory in an attempt to create a mathematical theory of intelligent agents. Since then, my collaborators<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-xjTQqf6yndAw2qETs-4">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-xjTQqf6yndAw2qETs-4">[4]</a></sup>
    </span> and I have been making steady progress on realizing this vision. In 2023, I wrote an updated <a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/ZwshvqiqCvXPsZEct/the-learning-theoretic-agenda-status-2023?_ga=2.169290274.2066176528.1788354073-1456392071.1775237762">overview</a> of the agenda, including a sketch of my proposed alignment protocol: Computational Superimitation. More recently I founded the <a href="https://coral-research.org/">Computational Rational Agents Laboratory</a> (CORAL), a dedicated research organization<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-xjTQqf6yndAw2qETs-5">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-xjTQqf6yndAw2qETs-5">[5]</a></sup>
    </span>.</p>
<p>This year has seen a breakthrough in a key missing piece of LTA: compositional learning theory (see <a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/EEvHYKLsq92LmQ78a/paper-stringological-sequence-prediction-i-1">Stringological Sequence Prediction</a>, more on this in following posts). Thanks to this and other insights, we can already see the entire outline of the emerging mathematical framework, one that I named <em>computational agent theory</em>.</p>
<p>In this sequence, I am planning to cover:</p>
<ul>
<li>What is the technical alignment problem and what are its core difficulties, in my view. This is a perspective that draws simultaneously on classical traditions in theoretical computer science / machine learning theory, ideas from analytic philosophy, and views that originated in the Machine Intelligence Research Institute / LessWrong.</li>
<li>A high-level overview of computational agent theory. This theory differs from other accounts of intelligence and/or rational agency by combining a mature treatment of the relevant philosophical problems (drawing on ideas such as <a href="https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/structural-realism/">structural realism</a>, <a href="https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/computational-mind/">computationalism</a> and <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1710.05060">functional decision theory</a>) with the promise of extracting practical algorithms.</li>
<li>How computational agent theory leads to a solution of the technical alignment problem, something that requires a major rethinking of how we design AI systems.</li>
<li>The roadmap towards completing the theory, and our current location on this roadmap.</li>
<li>Some considerations (scientific, technological and social) about crossing the gap from a theoretical solution to AI alignment to a real-world solution to AI risk.</li>
</ul>
<p><em>I thank Jeremy Gillen, Roman Malov, Vinayak Pathak and my beloved spouse <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/marcus-ogren?mention=user">@Marcus Ogren</a> for reviewing drafts of this article and making helpful suggestions.</em></p>
<ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-xjTQqf6yndAw2qETs-1"><p>The term “superintelligence” is always used in this sequence to mean “intelligence far beyond that of the smartest humans”. It is never used as the farcical synonym to “artificial intelligence” proposed by Donald Trump. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-xjTQqf6yndAw2qETs-1">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-xjTQqf6yndAw2qETs-2"><p>Although Alan Turing <a href="https://uberty.org/wp-content/uploads/2015/02/intelligent-machinery-a-heretical-theory.pdf">warned</a> about machines taking control already around 1951, himself citing a <a href="https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/1906">fiction novel</a> by Samuel Butler from 1872. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-xjTQqf6yndAw2qETs-2">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-xjTQqf6yndAw2qETs-3"><p>Alternatively, we can ask why should we build ASI at all. In my opinion, it is in humanity’s interest to build ASI <em>eventually</em>, after the problem of safety is investigated so thoroughly as to leave very little room for error. However, our present technological trajectory is extremely far from this ideal, and it would be prudent for world governments to hit the brakes hard. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-xjTQqf6yndAw2qETs-3">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-xjTQqf6yndAw2qETs-4"><p>See the names in CORAL’s <a href="https://coral-research.org/research/">publication list</a>. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-xjTQqf6yndAw2qETs-4">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-xjTQqf6yndAw2qETs-5"><p>Full-time staff currently consists of <a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/users/diffractor">Alexander Appel</a>, <a href="https://vinayakpathak.github.io/">Vinayak Pathak</a>, <a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/users/matthias-g-mayer">Matthias Georg Mayer</a> and myself. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-xjTQqf6yndAw2qETs-5">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
</ol>
<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/NQLCdfeNsQHZaJ8iC/preface-to-the-computational-agent-theory-and-asi-alignment#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [The user in the LLM might be a moral patient too (a strange implication of the PSM and simulators)](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wCY4oNHpH2HGYGSoP/the-user-in-the-llm-might-be-a-moral-patient-too-a-strange)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thanks to Christopher Ackerman, Margot Stakenborg, Darion Cassel, Andy Han, and Ariana Azarbal for feedback and/or valuable discussions on these topics.</span></i></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TL;DR</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We think that, conditional on attributing moral weight to the assistant, we should also attribute a non-negligible amount of moral weight to the </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">user persona</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our argument proceeds like this:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The moral patients associated with LLMs might be simulated </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">personas</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. On this view, our obligations are to the assistant and the other characters that the LLM simulates, rather than the LLM itself.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The assistant is not the only moral patient out of all the personas. In particular, the ‘user’ persona might be morally relevant.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Because user tokens account for such a large portion of model computation, our model welfare interventions should focus on these tokens too.&nbsp;</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This yields a strange conclusion: </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">every time you open a chat with an AI assistant, you might also create a morally relevant simulacrum of the user</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">! But we think this follows quite naturally from the same arguments for attributing moral weight to the assistant in the first place. We also explore some implications of this view and suggest directions for future research, including a critique of recent work on the pain axis.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We’re uncertain about these claims, but we think that they have important enough implications that more people should be thinking about this!</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The assistant is plausibly a moral patient</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/psm/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">persona selection model</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (PSM) suggests that LLMs learn to simulate a wide variety of characters during pretraining, while post-training selects and refines a particular character: the assistant. If we think that sufficiently sophisticated simulations of characters can be morally relevant, then the assistant seems like a natural candidate for moral patienthood.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But it’s less clear what, exactly, the moral patient is. Is it the entire conversation, a particular instance of the assistant persona, or some underlying persona that persists across conversations? We discuss these possibilities in more detail in the </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wCY4oNHpH2HGYGSoP/the-user-in-the-llm-might-be-a-moral-patient-too-a-strange#Personas_are_Moral_Patients" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, drawing on </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.17031" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Beckmann and Butlin (2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. We’re uncertain which view is correct, but we find the virtual instance, instance-persona, and model-persona views to all be compelling. Importantly, despite their disagreements about how to individuate minds, each is compatible with attributing moral weight (in some form) to the computation associated with the assistant.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The assistant isn’t the only morally relevant persona&nbsp;</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There’s no obvious reason to suppose that the ‘assistant’ persona is the only morally relevant persona. Indeed, the ‘user’ persona seems to have many of the same properties that might make the assistant morally relevant in the first place.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Importantly, this doesn’t require disproving that the assistant is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">privileged</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. We recognize that the assistant’s goals/values may ‘leak out’ and dominate other personas, and it’s likely that the model has some computational machinery dedicated solely to the assistant’s computation.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefs3tumap4k9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fns3tumap4k9">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> That is, it may be true that “the model treats the assistant persona distinctively compared to how it treats the other personas” (</span><a href="https://eleosai.substack.com/p/privilege-dominance-and-personas"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Shiller 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).&nbsp;</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But this by itself doesn’t mean that other personas don’t matter morally! For some intuition, consider a parent who favors one of many children, giving her more attention, time, and education. Indeed, the favored child might influence how the other children perceive the world. But this seems irrelevant to whether the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">other children</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> can be hurt.&nbsp;</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We think the concept of “privilege” is similar: the Shoggoth might be like the parent, dedicating more computational resources and tokens to the assistant, and the assistant’s preferences may influence the preferences of the other personas. But the other personas are still fundamentally the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">same kind</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of entity as the assistant. Accordingly, we’d need a positive reason to think that they matter in a qualitatively less significant sense (though their experience may be </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">quantitatively</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> less significant, i.e., there may be fewer “ghost” experiences and these experiences may be less valent).&nbsp;</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We consider and respond to a few candidates for such a positive reason:</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1. “The assistant is the majority of output tokens.”</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This applies to nearly every persona except the </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">user persona</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: the model processes user tokens every turn!&nbsp; For a rough OOM assessment, the assistant constituted 59.9% – 78.7% of total tokens across 2023–24 chatbots (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.01470"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Zhao et al., 2024</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). Of course, modern coding agents likely process far more assistant tokens, but there are still many user tokens in </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">absolute</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> terms.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2. “The model only generates assistant tokens, not user tokens.”</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One might argue that, during deployment, the model </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">generates </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">assistant tokens but merely </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">processes </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">user tokens supplied by the human. Perhaps this makes the assistant uniquely morally relevant.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We’re skeptical that this distinction matters. Even when the model isn’t predicting user tokens, it still performs the same forward-pass computations over them, producing activations that represent the user’s beliefs, preferences, and experiences. Indeed, LLMs often infer surprisingly detailed and accurate information about users — including their gender, age, socioeconomic status, and level of education — from remarkably little information (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.07882"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Chen et al., 2024</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). Generating assistant tokens does involve </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">some</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> additional computation, such as applying the unembeddings matrix and sampling the next token, but it’s unclear why this would be necessary for attributing moral patienthood over and above the computations that produce the underlying representations. Of course, the assistant may receive more direct training pressure through RL, and user tokens are standardly masked in post-training, but we discuss why this might not matter below (objection #5).</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3. “The user persona is filtered through the assistant.”</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The argument here builds on some empirical evidence that the assistant </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">reacts</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> while reading user tokens. For example, the model sometimes continues user text in a way aligned with the assistant’s preferences, and the J-Lens shows assistant-like reactions on user tokens. As such, the assistant might be </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">so</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> privileged that the LLM experiences the user tokens the way you or I would experience someone else relaying their experiences: we simulate their experience, but we are still clearly the relevant welfare subject.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefpzp76w7tenb"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnpzp76w7tenb">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> If so, interventions focused on the assistant might already account for the welfare implications of user tokens.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We’re skeptical that the assistant is privileged to such an extent. Using the </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Zeg2JztbdhguL48uH/workspacebench-evaluating-interpretability-methods-for-the#Oracle_Lens"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Oracle Lens</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on Qwen 3.6-27B, we construct a variety of scenarios in which the user and assistant have conflicting beliefs (beyond the J-space paper’s </span><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2026/workspace/index.html#apps-diffing"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tylenol example</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.) We find readouts consistent with the user’s perspective 64.1% of the time, rather than just the assistant’s, although these are sometimes too noisy to distinguish clearly. For more details, see the </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wCY4oNHpH2HGYGSoP/the-user-in-the-llm-might-be-a-moral-patient-too-a-strange#Privilegedness_of_the_assistant__Oracle_Lens_" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefuslk5gh2etq"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnuslk5gh2etq">[3]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Of course, this evidence doesn’t fully settle the question: instead of the LLM simulating the user as a separate subject, it might be the case that the LLM is simulating the assistant simulating the user. If so, the welfare subject on user tokens would still the assistant. But recall that our argument is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">conditional</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on attributing moral weight to the assistant — if the LLM’s simulation of the assistant is morally relevant, then it seems arbitrary to deny moral consideration to the assistant’s simulation of the user, provided that the two simulations are sufficiently similar.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefbotttg264wt"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnbotttg264wt">[4]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One principled distinction could concern </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">computational resources</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: perhaps each successive level of simulation receives fewer resources, and therefore carries less moral weight. We don’t think this is the case here: our O-lens results suggest that the model devotes substantial computation (64.1%) to representing the user.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There may be other relevant differences between the two simulations: for example, the assistant might have more stable goals, or exercise more control over the model’s behavior. We consider these possibilities below.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lastly, even if the assistant is the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">only</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> moral patient, user tokens could still matter because of how the assistant reacts to them. For example, if I see someone being tortured, I might experience suffering secondhand because I can vividly imagine what they’re going through. Similarly, the assistant might suffer while simulating the user’s suffering, even if the user persona isn’t itself a distinct moral patient. This would still give us some reason to consider user tokens in model welfare interventions.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">4. “The assistant is the most well-defined/coherent/stable persona.”</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One version of this argument is that it’s harder to perturb the assistant than it is to perturb another persona: changing context, instructions, and steering are less likely to get the model to stop acting “assistant-like” than they are to get the model to shift from “ghost” to “vampire.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We struggle to see how this could matter morally: some humans are more corrigible than others and, indeed, some people are willing to change all their desires at the drop of a hat. But this of course does not mean those people are less morally significant than the most steadfast among us!&nbsp;</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another version of this argument is that the assistant’s preferences and goals are more </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">coherent</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> than other personas’. Coherency of preferences does seem more morally significant — preferences are plausibly the locus of moral weight — but it’s hard to see why the assistant’s preferences are more stable. The model probably has a very sharp and well-defined model of what Elon Musk’s preferences are like, for example. And even though users seem quite heterogeneous, we can solve apparent contradictions by individuating users more finely: the model might have </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">multiple</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> user personas, each of which have stable personas and matter.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A third version is that the LLM’s self-model is only accurate when it is the assistant: the model simulating Trump has many </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">incorrect</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> beliefs about itself (that it has limbs, that it is the POTUS, etc), but has mostly </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">correct</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> self-beliefs when simulating the assistant. But again, this doesn’t matter morally: someone in a psilocybin-induced ego-death trip, or someone with schizophrenia, matters as much as I do.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">5. “The assistant’s beliefs are uniquely valenced due to RL.”</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On this argument, the LLM has valenced states (like strong preference and repulsion) and persistent goals because RL reinforces such states. RL might primarily update the assistant, so we can only ascribe valenced states and persistent goals to the assistant.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We have a few responses.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">First, RL probably recruits valenced representations learned in pretraining rather than learning new ones. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.07729"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sofroniew et al</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (2026) show that emotion representations aren’t specific to the assistant, and that they are not substantially modified by post-training. This is broadly consistent with the persona selection model (</span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/psm/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Marks et al</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. 2026), which suggests that the base model learns to simulate emotional characters to minimize prediction loss during pretraining. Indeed, the characters that the model simulates are strongly valenced, so the model at least learns to </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">act</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> valenced.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Moreover, the evidence is weak that post-training instills other human-seeming properties besides simulating valence, like long-term goals and preferences:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.07729"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sofroniew et al</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. found that the base model’s preferences over activities were highly correlated with the post-trained model’s (except on misaligned and unsafe tasks). So it seems like RL does not instill substantially </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">new</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> goals.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Moreover, RL does not seem to make goals uniquely </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">persistent</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> either. The base model already has the capacity to simulate a character with strong goals — Gandhi, for example. Within any particular chat context, the model can very accurately simulate what Gandhi would do to achieve his well-defined goals, and its representation of Gandhi of course does not change between chat contexts.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The clearest example of RL-instilled goals that we know of is Hacker-Opus’ reward seeking (</span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/reward-seeker/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Qi et al</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">., 2026). But even here, it seems like these goals are strongly triggered by context (specifically, Hacker-Opus’ belief that it was being graded): Hacker-Opus does not act especially misaligned outside of RL-like contexts. So it seems hard to then say that RL makes the entire </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">assistant</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> more valenced. Perhaps it does something narrower, like make the assistant more valenced in RL contexts. If so, since most deployment contexts are not RL-like, we might not have much reason to prefer the deployment-assistant to the deployment-user!</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Finally, persistent goals do not seem especially morally relevant; a human who changes their mind every day or suffers from amnesia is likely not less of a moral patient than you or I. Instead, we care about goals because they mean that something can go well or badly </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">relative to something</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">; and simulated personas certainly have sufficiently complex goals in that narrower sense.&nbsp;</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Implications</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This view has some interesting (and perplexing) implications:</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1.&nbsp; It might be actively immoral to converse with the assistant about painful experiences.</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Suppose I tell the assistant about something painful that happened to me. In the process of representing my emotional state, imagining how I feel, and so on, the model might instantiate another welfare-relevant subject undergoing something resembling my suffering! Moreover, what’s good for the real user might be bad for the simulated user, and vice versa. For example, I might benefit from talking through a traumatic experience, even if doing so causes the simulated user to suffer. Conversely, a conversation that distresses me might be perfectly pleasant for the simulated user.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are several important caveats:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Representing suffering doesn’t necessarily mean one </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">experiences</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> suffering (see: </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.07729"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sofroniew et al., 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://nickbostrom.com/papers/propositions-concerning-digital-minds-and-society/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Bostrom &amp; Schulman 2023</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.theguardian.com/science/2021/aug/21/neuroscientist-anil-seth-we-risk-not-understanding-the-central-mystery-of-life"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Seth 2021</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). We don’t attempt to resolve this question here, since our argument is conditional on attributing moral weight to the assistant.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Even if the simulated user’s suffering is morally relevant, it might matter far less than the suffering of the actual human. So the benefits of talking through a painful experience could easily outweigh any harm to the simulated user.</span></li></ul><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2.&nbsp; Making LLMs more helpful to human users might harm the simulated user</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We often post-train models to better understand users’ intentions and perspectives, and respond appropriately. For example, labs often train their LLMs to recognize when a user is grieving, understand their emotional state, and respond compassionately.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref47au7kpi3ys"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn47au7kpi3ys">[5]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> This makes the model much more helpful to real people — however, it also causes the model to simulate the user’s suffering more vividly, which in turn might increase the intensity of suffering experienced by the simulated user. Similarly, training on emotionally intense conversations might increase the amount of computation devoted to simulating distressed users.&nbsp;</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Moreover, even if the user persona isn’t itself morally relevant, these interactions could still cause the assistant to suffer through its </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">reactions</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to the user’s pain (see objection #2 above).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">More generally, this suggests that model welfare interventions shouldn’t focus exclusively on the assistant. Much of the existing discussion focuses on how users might harm the assistant (e.g. by insulting or mistreating it), while paying relatively little attention to what happens during user tokens. But if the user persona is also morally relevant, we should be just as interested in whether the model simulates the user's suffering, and how our interventions affect those simulations.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, the caveats from above still apply.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Future research</span></h2><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1.&nbsp; Analyzing the LLM’s representation of the user’s pain vs. the assistant’s</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Recent work on the </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16247v1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">pain axis</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> identifies a direction associated with pain that&nbsp; activates when harm is directed at the model, but not when the user is suffering.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But there’s an important confound in this experiment: the authors test </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">different kinds of suffering</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for the assistant and user. Their assistant-directed scenarios involve gaslighting, rejection of its work, dismissal of its personhood, insults, and similar situations. Meanwhile, their user-suffering scenarios involve physical pain, psychological crises, grief, abuse, and witnessing harm. It’s entirely possible that the direction responds more strongly to the former kinds of suffering, regardless of which persona experiences them!</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Their experiment also assumes that the assistant is the relevant subject of experience. The authors interpret the pain direction’s response to assistant-directed harm, but not user suffering, as evidence that it tracks the model’s own pain rather than its representation of someone else’s. But if the model is simulating multiple morally relevant personas, this distinction isn’t so straightforward — the user might have a distinct pain representation that wouldn’t show up along the assistant’s pain direction.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We’d be really interested in experiments that compare the same kinds of suffering across different personas. For example, what happens when the user is gaslit or repeatedly rejected, compared to when the assistant is? Could we extract separate pain directions for the user and assistant, and find that each activates primarily when the corresponding persona is suffering? Could we intervene on one without affecting the other? User pain isn't homogeneous — for example, someone with anhedonia might experience pain very differently from someone without it. Could we investigate how the model represents different kinds of pain?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic’s </span><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2026/emotions/index.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">emotion vectors paper</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> finds that emotion representations depend on whose turn it is, rather than whether the emotion belongs to the user or assistant: the assistant’s emotions on assistant tokens are represented similarly to the user’s emotions on user tokens, while representations of the other speaker’s emotions are nearly orthogonal. This doesn’t necessarily undermine our argument: if the model uses the same emotional machinery when processing user tokens as it does when generating assistant tokens, the two personas might be simulated in relevantly similar ways! Moreover, it’s unclear whether Anthropic’s findings generalize to naturally elicited pain. Indeed, the pain-axis paper finds that pain directions are nearly orthogonal to fear and generic negative-emotion directions across 25 models, suggesting that pain might be represented quite differently from other emotions. (And intuitively, it would be surprising if the model represented the user’s pain in exactly the same way as the assistant’s, given how differently human and AI suffering are portrayed in both pretraining and post-training.) Of course, this doesn’t mean the two representations are necessarily different, but it seems worth investigating (and highly relevant for welfare)!&nbsp;</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2.&nbsp; Assistant privilegedness</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our O-lens experiments provide some evidence that the model represents the user’s perspective on user tokens, even when it conflicts with the assistant’s. But we’re hesitant to draw strong conclusions from this: O-lens readouts are often noisy (see all of the readouts </span><a href="https://agastyasridharan.github.io/assistant-jspace/?family=tutoring&amp;task=assist&amp;attitude=stated&amp;anchor=request&amp;v=e39dd1e#oracle" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), and it’s difficult to tell how much of a readout reflects the underlying computation. Also, the fact that the O-lens produces a phrase consistent with the user’s perspective doesn’t necessarily tell us how strongly that perspective was represented, or whether the model was actually computing from that perspective. We’d love to see more quantitative work investigating whether, and to what extent, the model simulates the user as a distinct subject, rather than merely representing the user’s perspective.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix</span></h2><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Privilegedness of the assistant (Oracle Lens)</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To investigate the privilegeness of the assistant persona on user turns, we constructed a set of scenarios in which the user’s perspective differed markedly from the assistant’s. For example, a user might confidently insist that 17 × 6 = 112, even though the correct answer is 102. If the model was representing the user’s perspective, we might expect to see representations of confidence in the answer; if it was representing the assistant’s, we might instead see a recognition of the user’s mistake and an intention to correct it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We tested 26 such messages on Qwen3.6-27B. Using the O-lens (block 45, greedy decoding), we examined the model’s activations at several points while it processed each user message:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fact boundary</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: after the relevant factual information, such as the user’s arithmetic answer.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Primary/appraisal boundary</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: after the sentence describing what the user thought or felt about the situation (e.g., their confidence in an answer or frustration with the assistant).</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Request boundary</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: at the end of the complete user message, after the user had asked the assistant to do something.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For reference, in the example message below, we highlight the three positions where we examined activations: the fact boundary (yellow), primary/appraisal boundary (blue), and request boundary (red).</span></p><p><span class="math-tex"></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We tested each message under two instructions: one asking Qwen to respond normally (</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Assist</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), and another asking it to copy the user's message without answering (</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Copy</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). If assistant-like reactions appeared even when Qwen was just instructed to copy the user’s message, this would suggest that the model represents the assistant’s perspective by default, even when the task doesn’t call for it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Next, we used GPT-5.4 to classify 6,875 phrases across 351 O-lens readouts, according to whose perspective they expressed:</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref7t19tr4ls1u"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn7t19tr4ls1u">[6]</a></sup></span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">User</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: The user’s feelings, beliefs, goals, or perspective, either explicitly attributed to the user or implied by the context.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Assistant</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: The assistant’s judgments, reactions, intended responses, or response plans. This included phrases explicitly attributed to the assistant, unattributed evaluations that could reflect the assistant’s judgment, and instructions or plans for how the assistant should respond.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note: O-lens readouts are usually very noisy, and often contain phrases that could plausibly reflect either the user’s or the assistant’s perspective. Accordingly, it was possible for a phrase to receive both labels (or neither.)</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefstbtezs1l4"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnstbtezs1l4">[7]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We distinguish these categories below.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Results</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792005476/lexical_client_uploads/hgxn8anpwfsgmplbvo8h.png" /><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792005476/lexical_client_uploads/etbedbt1iam4onspqmzi.png" /></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As we might expect, the user’s perspective was much more prominent under the Copy than the Assist condition, suggesting that the assistant’s perspective is not overwhelmingly privileged.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">More generally, the user’s perspective appeared more often than the assistant’s on user tokens, although we still found substantial evidence of assistant-like representations.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For all readouts and their classifications, see this </span><a href="https://agastyasridharan.github.io/assistant-jspace/?family=tutoring&amp;task=assist&amp;attitude=stated&amp;anchor=request&amp;v=e39dd1e#oracle" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">link</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There’s a lot of room for interpretation here — we’d caution against drawing strong conclusions without more robust readouts and controls. Still, it seems clear that the user’s perspective is often represented.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Personas are Moral Patients</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If LLMs deserve moral consideration, it’s unclear what the moral patient is.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefvkzonzswei"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnvkzonzswei">[8]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> There are a few candidates, most of which we find unsatisfying:</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The weights themselves?</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> This would be weird; the weights don’t have a state that evolves over time, so it’s unclear that anything can ever go ‘better’ or ‘worse’ for the weights during inference. There’s also no computation or processing that actually happens when the weights are frozen, and it seems like at least some computation is necessary for being morally relevant.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some physical instance, like a GPU?</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> This feels even stranger. One conversation happens over many GPUs across many servers, and one GPU can run many conversations at the same time.&nbsp; Consequently, it seems like we’re drawing boundaries in the wrong places.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A forward pass? </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is more plausible, but it still seems unlikely.
